Bear Grylls Bought a ‘Derelict’ Island With ‘No Power’ and ‘Rats’ at 25 Instead of a London Apartment—Here’s How It Looks Decades Later

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Bear Grylls made an unusual decision decades ago, when he acquired a marital starter home with his wife, Shara Grylls.

“At 25, just married, we bought a small, abandoned, off-grid island from an ad in the back of a magazine,” the British adventurer, 52, wrote in a Nov. 3 Instagram caption.

Grylls revealed that the purchase of St. Tudwal’s Island West cost them around $120,000.

“There were many who thought us crazy,” he continued. “The best adventures start with nay-sayers. Thank you Shara for being brave and saying yes!”

While the comment section had users divided over the relatability of purchasing an entire island—with one commenter wondering how “celebrities get so far out of touch from reality and regular people”—there were also a handful of people who stepped in to point out that even when adjusted for inflation, the island was less costly than the average U.S. home.

The “Running Wild With Bear Grylls” star explained the decision to the Sunday Times in 2021, noting that snapping up a Welsh island seemed like a more exciting use of the couple’s money.

“In 2001, we were going to buy a London flat for $288,000, but we bought a houseboat on the Thames [now sold] and the island together, and it cost less than the apartment. So we decided on the path less trodden and an amazing adventure,” he said.

Grylls shed some light on just how dilapidated the property was 25 years ago.

“The island had a 200-year-old lighthouse and two lightkeeper’s cottages, but [the houses] were derelict with no power, no water, no roof, and rats,” he said. “I remember my first bath sitting in 3 inches of water pumped from the old tanks we’d cleaned out. There was rubble everywhere and rain coming through, but it was a great moment.”

Considering Grylls has made a career out of his ability to survive in extremely harsh conditions, it’s not entirely surprising that he was able to get through a few rough years before the island would become a more suitable dwelling.

It took a decade to get permission to build a slipway, which Grylls said they finished just a few years ago.

“It has been life-changing for us,” he said of the addition. “Previously we had to keep a boat on a mooring, row ashore, and walk a mile up the beach with all the shopping.”

Eventually, the two lightkeeper’s cottages were combined into a single home with four bedrooms. But the island is still off-grid.

“It’s 20 acres and 5 miles offshore with no mains, electricity, or running water,” Grylls told Vanity Fair in 2012. “It has a little lighthouse beside our house and is surrounded by amazing sea cliffs, seals, and dolphins. It is my favorite place on the planet!”

To travel to the destination, Grylls and his family take a “30-foot, high-speed ex-lifeboat,” which he described as a “rigid inflatable boat with twin 300-horsepower engines behind it that can tackle any sea it needs to.”

He said the house is “as tough as you get.” Thanks to “2-foot-thick old limestone walls,” it retains heat in the winter and stays cool in the summer.

“But when those gales come in, you can still feel it almost shake,” Grylls admitted. “Sea spray reaches the windows despite the 150-foot elevation.”

While St. Tudwal’s may lack some of the modern amenities guests might typically expect, the Grylls family does “sometimes have friends” visit.

“They need to be up for an adventure and prepared to get stuck there if the weather turns,” he said.

Grylls has recently made headlines for his connection to disgraced former comic Russell Brand, who was recently found guilty of one count of rape and three counts of sexual assault.

Grylls helped baptize Brand in a ceremony in the River Thames in 2024, after the allegations against the actor had come to light. Prior to the baptism, Brand appeared in a 2023 episode of Grylls’ show, in which the pair traveled across the Hebrides Islands.

“Brand’s faith seemed genuine to me at the time when I was a guest at his private baptism,” Grylls wrote in a Nov. 6 X post. “I have attended a few now. Even visiting in prisons with people who have committed terrible crimes. It’s part of our calling as men and women of faith, to love and serve anyone. It is the sick that need healing.”

Noting that “judgment is for the courts and God,” Grylls added that “it is right that justice has now been served.

“I can’t imagine how hard this time has been for the victims, the heroes of the day,” he said.

Hannah Chambers is a senior celebrity and lifestyle reporter who joined Realtor.com in 2026. She has been writing entertainment and lifestyle content since 2016, with her work appearing in Cosmopolitan, BravoTV, NYLON, Bustle, Elite Daily, and The Zoe Report, among other publications. Hannah received a bachelor’s degree in journalism from The University of Maine. In her spare time, she loves to chat about pop culture and is a major fan of reality TV.

I Thought Multi-Tools Were a Gimmick — Then I Took One on a Camping Trip

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

If, like me, you think multi-tools are gimmicky gadgets, you might change your mind once you discover how handy they are when your toolbox isn’t within reach.

Our editors and experts handpick every product we feature. We may earn a commission from your purchases.Learn more.

I admit I scoffed when my partner first started raving about his Leatherman Wave+ multi-tool. I thought it looked like an expensive, over-elaborate gadget that was destined to gather dust in a junk drawer.

However, as a keen hiker and camper, I’ve discovered how useful it is to have pliers, knives, a saw, scissors, files and other tools packed into one compact, quality piece of kit. I’ve also watched my tech-obsessed partner regularly reach for its precision tools when tinkering with his electrical equipment.

Ahead, I’ll share how a tool I once dismissed as a gimmick earned a permanent spot in my pack, and what two experts think makes a Leatherman worth the price tag.

I had to realize that our Leatherman Wave+ isn’t meant to replace a well-stocked toolbox to understand its value. Instead, it fills the gap when dedicated tools aren’t conveniently available or really needed.

At home, I regularly use the tool’s tiny scissors to carefully prune dead leaves from our many houseplants, the knife to open packages and the small screwdriver to tighten the legs on my glasses. My partner is an IT professional who also has an impressive collection of synthesizers, and he regularly reaches for the tool to make small technical repairs and splice wires.

I’ve found the Wave+ even more useful when we’re borrowing my parents’ campervan, hiking, or camping. The bottle opener is handy for those after-adventure beers, the serrated knife slices baguettes for trailside sandwiches, the saw cuts small, dry branches for kindling, and the screwdriver has adjusted our portable stove and tightened folding chairs.

The pocket-sized multi-tool packs 18 functions into a stainless-steel form that measures just four inches when closed and weighs 8.5 ounces. Alongside the tools I’ve mentioned, it has pliers, bit drivers, a wire stripper and crimper, a ruler, an extra knife blade and file, and an admittedly slightly-tricky-to-use can opener.

While I don’t regularly use all 18 tools, and I might never use some, having the selection provides peace of mind, especially in remote places.

Daniel Kilburn, founder of Emergency Action Planning and a preparedness expert, has carried his Leatherman Surge since his military service in 2006. “It’s an expedient tool to have on hand when you know you’re going to be someplace where you won’t have access to any others,” he says.

Scott Emanuel of knives, multi-tools, and outdoor equipment retailer Heinnie Haynes is also a multi-tool convert. He appreciated having one for quick fixes while renovating his cottage, and particularly rates the needle-nose pliers, which rival standard models rather than acting as an emergency substitute.

Kilburn suggests multi-tools can be a useful starting point for someone who doesn’t own a toolbox, and at the other end of the spectrum are the tinkerers and prepared problem-solvers Emanuel sees as natural fits.

“The person who will value a Leatherman is the one who spends their after-hours in the garage building stuff that they probably never need, it’s the kind of self-reliant and prepared person who you rely on to solve a problem,” Emanuel says.

The Leatherman Wave+ currently costs $129.95, meaning it should be a well-considered purchase rather than an impulse buy.

It’s reassuring to know that you’ll be investing in a refined product designed because of a genuine practical need. Founder Tim Leatherman conceived a pliers-based multi-tool after encountering car and plumbing problems while traveling through Europe in the 1970s, and after careful development, he launched the original Pocket Survival Tool in 1983.

Today you’re paying for that established reputation and durable construction, and Leatherman backs its products with a 25-year warranty.

“They’re ridiculously well built, really well regarded, and their choice of tools is good,” Emanuel says. He describes sought-after Leathermans as the “collectible top end of the multi-tool market.”

Kilburn recalls seeing lower-priced multi-tools failing his comrades during their military career. “Some disintegrate over time because they were just cheaply made with poor-quality material [and] poor-quality steel,” he says. His Leatherman Surge, meanwhile, has lasted over 20 years. If replacing it again, he says he would choose Leatherman over his backup Gerber multi-tool because he prefers its feel and durability.

Leatherman makes a wide range of multi-tools, so it pays to consider which functions you’ll genuinely use. The Signal model adds outdoor-focused features suited to camping, including a ferrocerium rod, emergency whistle and sharpener, but omits scissors. I use scissors enough for plant pruning, first aid and gear repairs that the Wave+ remains the better fit for me.

If you’re unlikely to use most of its functions, a smaller, cheaper multi-tool could make more sense. The Wave+ is also heavier than minimalist models, and there’s a learning curve when figuring out where everything lives.

Don’t forget that a premium multi-tool only represents good value if you look after it. We remove dirt after outdoor adventures and dry and lubricate it before storing. We also avoid forcing it to tackle jobs better suited to full-size tools. I wouldn’t, for example, choose its compact saw over a dedicated full-size version for pruning substantial tree branches in my yard.

If you’re using a Wave+ in the UK, like me, be aware of the country’s knife laws. You need a “good reason” to carry it in public because its blades lock. However, Emanuel explains that the legal language is ambiguous, so you shouldn’t assume you can carry one wherever you go just because it’s part of a multi-tool.

The Wave+ has convinced me that a good multi-tool is more than merely an expensive gimmick. It may not replace dedicated tools, but having so many functions at my fingertips in a form that is so compact, portable and easy to use makes it a practical, worthwhile investment.

The Wave Plus is a multi-tool workhorse. It packs 18 tools in one, including sharp blades, spring scissors, wire cutters, and more.

US Treasury walks back crypto surveillance ‘travel rule’ proposal

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

FinCEN and the Federal Reserve Board of Governors rescinded the "Travel Rule," which was first proposed in 2020 to require financial institutions to collect information on funds worth $250 or more.

The Treasury Department has withdrawn a controversial proposal that would have expanded reporting requirements for cryptocurrency transactions.

On Friday, the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network and the Federal Reserve Board of Governors rescinded the "Travel Rule," which was first proposed in 2020 to require financial institutions to collect information on transactions of $250 or more, decreasing that threshold from $3,000.

In a filing, FinCEN said it was withdrawing the proposed rule because more than five years had passed, the convertible virtual currency industry had changed and additional public input would be helpful.

"The Agencies believe that any future, potential amendments to the Recordkeeping Rule and Travel Rule related to CVC should be considered in light of these recent developments to ensure that such amendments are fit for purpose," FinCEN said.

When the rule was proposed, FinCEN had argued that bad actors were increasingly using smaller transactions to move illicit funds. However, crypto groups, like Coin Center, had warned that lowering the threshold from $3,000 to $250 would mean that more ordinary transactions would be subject to "warrantless financial surveillance."

In a blog on Friday, Coin Center said it was happy to see the rule proposal rescinded, along with another rule proposal that was withdrawn last month that would have imposed reporting and recordkeeping requirements on transactions involving self-hosted wallets.

"This follows Treasury’s withdrawal last month of the proposed 'unhosted wallet' and cryptocurrency mixing rules, both of which Coin Center had also opposed," the organization said. 'Taken together, these withdrawals close the books on several long-pending proposals that would have expanded the Bank Secrecy Act’s already extensive financial surveillance regime."

Crypto Council for Innovation CEO Ji Hun Kim said in a statement that withdrawing the travel rule was the "right call."

"It had not moved forward over the past several years for good reason," he said. "Lowering the threshold from $3,000 to $250 for cross-border transfers would have placed undue burden on institutions."

Binance hack is ‘cyber war,’ TRM Labs executive says

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

TRM Labs executive Ari Redbord called for governments to take offensive action against North Korean hackers following the record $6.3 billion Binance theft.

The $6.3 billion Binance hack sent shockwaves through the crypto industry. Following a series of increasingly severe cybersecurity incidents, the attack on the world’s biggest crypto exchange demonstrated how far state-backed hackers can, and will, go.

TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence company, was on the front lines of efforts to stop the theft, releasing a report in its aftermath. The firm was early to attribute the attack to the North Korean hacking group, TraitorTrader, citing “indisputable” onchain and offchain evidence. The $1.5 billion hack of Bybit in February 2025 was attributed to the same group.

Binance said in its postmortem on Nov. 3 that attackers posed as recruiters to plant malware on an engineer’s work computer, then used a compromised internal AI tool to reach its wallet-signing service.

Ari Redbord, TRM Labs’ global head of policy and government affairs, told Cointelegraph that although the industry’s coordinated response to the attack was unprecedented, “we are only going to see much more of this.”

The speed and scale of industry’s response to the hack were among the factors that stood out to Redbord.

Tether froze 971.1 million USDt (USDT) tokens just 37 minutes after the main sweep. Combined, stablecoin issuers froze tokens worth about $2.67 billion, according to TRM. “Nothing like that has ever been done before,” Redbord said. “They were able to claw back 40% right out the gate.”

The race to catch stolen funds before hackers can move them offchain is a critical part of defending against such attacks. TRM’s Redbord explained that the Beacon Network, an intelligence-sharing system TRM launched in 2025, allowed companies to coordinate quickly to immobilize the funds before working to seize them. “Normally I see a few Beacon alerts maybe every day. I’ve seen, I don’t know, hundreds or maybe even a thousand Beacon alerts over the last few days,” he said.

Related: Ethereum devs won’t fork Glamsterdam on Hoodi until smart contract risks resolved

The push for coordination comes as Bloomberg reported on Nov. 4 that the T3 Financial Crime Unit, which TRM formed with Tron and Tether in September 2024, disbanded in late August, with sources citing slow responses and poor coordination. Tether said it would continue working with TRM through Beacon.

Although Redbord called for greater coordination, he warned that such efforts may do little to deter future attacks. He noted that North Korea’s Oct. 29 theft was equivalent to roughly a third of the country’s GDP and accounted for half of the $12.5 billion stolen by state-backed hackers since 2017. Even if attackers recover only $1 billion, the potential payoff remains enormous, he argued.

“North Korea will keep doing this as long as it’s effective,” he said.

Redbord argued that stopping such attacks requires offensive action from governments in response to North Korea’s use of hackers operating as an arm of the state.

TRM’s capacity gap report found that the gap between attackers and cybersecurity defenders continues to widen, driven by hackers’ early adoption of frontier technologies such as AI. He said law enforcement and compliance teams need AI to find and patch vulnerabilities as quickly as hackers can exploit them.

“Every time North Korea hacks, we should be hacking them back,” he continued, calling for offensive cyber operations and action against laundering networks and banks that hackers use to cash out. “This is cyber war,” he said.

Please Do Not Buy Costco’s $520 Canon R100 Kit

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Please Do Not Buy Costco’s $520 Canon R100 Kit

The holiday season is ramping up and that once again means Canon is trotting out the same cash cow it has milked for the last three straight years. Costco (soon to be followed by other stores, surely) is offering a Canon EOS R100 kit for an enticing $520. Do not buy it.

Consider this a public service announcement, and a reiteration of the same plea I published two years ago (except, somehow, the R100 is more expensive now!). The only reason to buy the Canon EOS R100 is that it is cheap. Like, cheap cheap. It is easily the most affordable interchangeable lens mirrorless camera that you can buy brand-new right now, but that is quite literally the only thing it has going for it.

As an investment in someone’s future in photography, it is a terrible purchase.

“Using the R100 felt like going a few steps backward in camera technology. Yes, it’s simple in its intention and design, but maybe a little too simple,” Chris Niccolls wrote in his review. “Even significantly older cameras than the R100 outperform it. I don’t think I would recommend this camera to many people unless the budget had to remain absolutely fixed at sub-$500. Even then, I would probably just stick to my smartphone.”

We've been particularly harsh on what we call "toy cameras" lately, and those are basically any compact camera that repurposes what are glorified dashcam sensors and crams them into plastic camera-shaped bodies. Those are e-waste waiting to happen. The R100 is not e-waste in the sense that it is a camera that does take decent photos and features a removable battery. It is better than a piece of garbage, but the benchmark for a $500+ purchase should be significantly higher than "not garbage." There are better options out there that are within the same price tier.

While the R100 wins as the cheapest, there are three cameras that are better that can be purchased brand-new today for around the same price.

82 PixelMeter Nikon Z30 Reviewer Consensus: Strong The Nikon Z30 is a compact, user-friendly mirrorless camera designed primarily for vloggers and content creators who prioritize video production. While it excels at capturing sharp 4K video with reliable autofocus and a convenient vari-angle screen, it lacks an electronic viewfinder and in-body stabilization, making it less suitable for dedicated stills photography. Nikon Z30Buy new on B&HNikon Z30Buy used on KEH.com

First, let's consider the Nikon Z30. While it isn't perfect, it is a substantially better camera. While it does not have an electronic viewfinder, unlike the R100, it does feature a fully articulating rear touchscreen, is smaller and lighter than the R100, takes great video, and has excellent autofocus performance. It has a faster burst photo mode than the R100, has a good degree of ruggedness to it, and is overall a better feeling and operating camera. The Z30 is also substantially discounted versus its launch price: it costs under $600 now.

82 PixelMeter Canon EOS R50 Reviewer Consensus: Strong The Canon EOS R50 is a compact, entry-level mirrorless camera that offers impressive subject-tracking autofocus and sharp 4K video capabilities for beginners. While it serves as an excellent, user-friendly tool for content creators, its small form factor results in limited physical controls and a lack of in-body image stabilization. Canon EOS R50Buy new on B&HCanon EOS R50Buy used on KEH.com

Admittedly, the Z30 isn't the best photo-specific camera, so if that is more important to you when trying to find something better than the R100, then Canon's own R50 is just overall a better camera in every way. Everything that's wrong with the R100 is better on the R50: you get back an EVF, you get a fully articulating rear LCD, improved video features, and -- really importantly -- the R50 supports USB-C charging directly through the camera. One of the biggest drawbacks of the R100 is its lack of support for this, which makes traveling with the camera a hassle since you always have to carry the wall charger with you. The Canon R50 is available today for $679.

84 PixelMeter Canon EOS R50 V Reviewer Consensus: Strong The Canon EOS R50 V is a compact, video-centric mirrorless camera that provides creators with high-quality 4K output, 10-bit color, and convenient vertical shooting tools. Although it lacks in-body image stabilization and an electronic viewfinder, its reliable autofocus and accessible controls make it a strong entry point for those upgrading from smartphones. Canon EOS R50 VBuy new on B&HCanon EOS R50 VBuy used on KEH.com

The R50V, which is a video-focused version of the R50, is also a great choice if you want to stick with Canon but won't miss a viewfinder and want to get even closer to the R100's price point. The R50V is basically the R50 with its EVF chopped off, and for that, it's available for $569.

I will admit, it is impossible to buy any other mirrorless camera brand-new that comes with a lens and memory card for $520. The lens Canon bundles with the R100 isn't good, but to get it separately costs $350. That means the R100 actually is worth just $170 as part of this kit. Again, this camera's only selling point is that it is cheap as dirt. But I beg you to find something that's affordable and also good.

Right now at KEH, you can get a Canon 5D Mark II in excellent condition for $296. You can then pick up a 24-105mm f/4L for $475 or the more entry-level option of 24-105mm f/3.5-5.6 for $256. Both options are slightly more expensive than Costco's kit, but are substantially better combos that will actually provide a burgeoning photographer with the ability to learn and grow. There are so many other possibilities on the used market, too. This is just one example.

Canon 5D Mark IIBuy used on KEH.comCanon EF 24-105mm f/4 L IS USMBuy used on KEH.comCanon EF 24-105mm f/3.5-5.6 IS STMBuy used on KEH.com

This all takes me back to my real main issue with the R100. It's barely fine as far as technology goes, but it is so limited that it serves as a poor camera to invest anything more than a first glance into the world of photography with. It cannot grow with someone beyond the very first stage of photography and after that, they're going to want one of these other cameras I've mentioned that costs just a little bit more. Why spend that amount twice? Skip the R100 stage and go for a camera that can support several years of interest, not just a few weeks.

I love photography. It would break my heart if the reason someone didn't realize they loved it too was because the experience of the R100 was so bland and uninteresting that they gave up on the hobby before it had time to take root.

Greece Passes Crypto Tax Law With 10% Gains Levy, 15% on Staking and DeFi

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Greece Passes Crypto Tax Law With 10% Gains Levy, 15% on Staking and DeFi

Greece's parliament passed a bill Thursday imposing a 10% capital-gains tax on cryptocurrency profits, marking a shift from a proposal discussed in October to legislation approved by lawmakers.

The measure also puts a 15% tax on income from staking, crypto lending and decentralized finance, or DeFi.

The legislation’s crypto provisions sit inside a wider Finance Ministry package dealing with debt servicing, financial oversight, and government accounting, with the new crypto income-tax rules scheduled for January 1, 2027.

Individual investors with annual net crypto gains of €500 ($557) or less will be exempt. Cross that threshold, though, and the 10% tax applies to the entire gain, rather than just the amount over €500 ($557).

For example, an investor with €600 ($669) in net gains would face a €60 ($67) tax under the new rate, not €10 ($11). The rules generally calculate gains by subtracting the acquisition cost from the value received when the crypto is disposed of.

Selling Bitcoin for euros is one taxable event, but spending crypto on goods or services can also count as a disposal. Investors will need transaction records to establish what they paid for their assets and what those assets were worth when sold or spent.

Yield from staking, lending, and providing liquidity to DeFi protocols is treated differently. Greece will classify those returns as interest income and tax them at 15%, according to the Finance Ministry.

Qualifying crypto losses exceeding €500 ($557) can be carried forward for five years and offset against future crypto gains. The bill also gives investors a 12-month window to voluntarily declare previously unreported crypto income without late-filing penalties or interest, provided they meet its conditions and pay within 60 days of filing.

The passage follows an October public consultation on the proposal.The draft proposed a 10% rate that was already lower than the 15% levy Greek officials had discussed in June.

Athens says exchanging one cryptocurrency for another should not itself trigger a capital-gains tax. But the wording lawmakers approved may not make that exclusion as clear as the government intended.

A review by parliament's Scientific Service flagged the bill's broad language covering disposals made "for consideration." That phrase could include token-to-token trades, the review said, even though the accompanying explanation says such swaps should not create taxable gains.

The reviewers recommended an explicit exemption and suggested clearer rules for exchanges involving stablecoins and electronic-money tokens. Those details matter for traders who rotate between assets without cashing out to fiat currency.

Greece is also preparing the administrative rules needed to put the tax into practice. A senior Finance Ministry official told Reuters that guidance on transaction transparency and tax audits would be issued in the coming weeks.

The government has not estimated how much the tax will raise. Officials say the market is difficult to measure because many Greek crypto investors use trading platforms based overseas.

Supreme Court agrees to weigh in on Goldman Sachs arbitration dispute

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

The Supreme Court on Friday afternoon added a new case to its oral argument docket for the 2026-27 term, granting a petition for review filed by Goldman Sachs Bank in a case over whether and when a bankruptcy court can override the Federal Arbitration Act and refuse to honor an agreement to arbitrate disputes.

The dispute in which the justices agreed to weigh in, Goldman Sachs Bank USA v. Brown, began three years ago, when Rhea Brown and Gregory Maze filed for bankruptcy. Both listed credit card debts with Goldman Sachs Bank as one of their debts.

The Bankruptcy Code imposes an automatic stay, which (among other things) bars creditors from attempting to collect debts from someone who has filed for bankruptcy. Because Goldman Sachs, according to Maze and Brown, repeatedly continued to try to seek payment of their credit card debts, they filed an adversary proceeding (that is, a separate lawsuit within the broader bankruptcy case) against the bank, in which they contended that the bank had willfully violated the automatic stay provision.

Pointing to an arbitration clause in their credit card agreements, Goldman Sachs asked the bankruptcy court to put the adversary proceeding on hold and require Brown and Maze to arbitrate their dispute. The bankruptcy court denied that request, and both the federal district court and the U.S. Court of Appeals for the 4th Circuit upheld that ruling. A divided three-judge panel of the 4th Circuit concluded that requiring Brown and Maze to arbitrate their claim “would interfere and conflict with the strong and established policies and purposes of the Bankruptcy Code.”

Goldman Sachs came to the Supreme Court in June, asking the justices to take up its case. It told the court that the 4th Circuit’s ruling squarely conflicted with a decision by the U.S. Court of Appeals for the 2nd Circuit. Moreover, it added, “[t]he practical stakes of these issues are enormous” because of the ubiquity of arbitration clauses in consumer credit agreements. The 4th Circuit’s decision, it said, “imperils the enforceability of arbitration agreements in many of them.”

After considering Goldman Sachs’ petition for review at four consecutive conferences, the justices granted it on Friday afternoon. The case likely will be argued sometime next year, with a decision to follow by late June or early July.

More orders from the justices’ private conference on Friday are expected at 9:30 a.m. EST on Monday.

Amy Howe is the co-founder of SCOTUSblog and its primary reporter.

10 Zelda: Ocarina Of Time Tips For The First-Time Player

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Nintendo Switch 2The Legend of ZeldaThe Legend of Zelda: Ocarina Of Time

A happy Zelda weekend to you all! While a lot of us are going into this latest Zelda release confident and comfortable in our ancient (or, in my case, recent) knowledge of how to play Ocarina of Time, this game is 28 whole years old and there are many, many people who have never played it, don’t remember it, or weren’t even alive when it came out. And whew, this is a very different Zelda game than some of the more recent ones!

So I put together some tips for you all. Hopefully at least a few of them help you have a smoother adventure in Hyrule this weekend. Enjoy the ride, folks, this one’s a hell of a classic:

In Kokiri Forest, when you first get control of Link, you can immediately head out of your house and down the path to the left to reach the Know-It-Alls. The characters inside the house will give you some useful tips, while the fellow outside will show you some basic combat moves including rolling and dodging. He’ll then point you in the direction of a sword, after which point you can return and learn how to do the mighty jump attack (more on that momentarily). These are (almost) all of the combat moves you’ll need to know for the whole game, so it’s good to master them early.

The jump attack is powerful, doing double the damage of a regular swing. It’s also a little tricky to execute, requiring two button inputs instead of just one…seemingly. You can actually also do this move by tilting forward on the control stick (while targeting) and just pressing A. I recommend doing the attack this way, and I recommend using this attack aÂ lot.

Everyone in Hyrule wants you to follow the main story path. They are, in fact, very worried you’ll miss it. So you’ll constantly hear NPCs directing you: go to the Great Deku Tree! Go to Hyrule Castle! Well…maybe don’t. At least, not right away. Ocarina of Time has tons of secrets, items, and just generally stuff to do that isn’t on the main path, so don’t feel obligated to stick to it. Even in Kokiri Forest, there are extra items you can obtain early if you just wander around for a while. You also can (and should!) backtrack to areas you’ve previously visited at just about any time!

Once you reach Castle Town, you’ll start to run into minigames. Ocarina of Time has a lot of them, and many are located in shops off the square. In some Zelda games, you don’t really need to do the minigames on offer, and technically that’s true in this one. But I recommend you at least give them a whirl. Some of them have great rewards, like bigger storage bags for certain items, or empty bottles. Don’t be afraid to goof off a little on your adventure!

Which, speaking of empty bottles. these are by far your most precious resource. Get as many as you can as soon as you can. If someone’s got a bottle for you, drop everything and do what they say. Empty bottles are used to hold all sorts of things: items you need for quests and to unlock secret paths, potions to refill your health, and fairies that can revive you automatically if you die. There are a total of five empty bottles in the game: try to get them all!

It can be tempting to just hack and slash your way to victory in Ocarina of Time, but some enemies aren’t so easily taken down. If you’re struggling, it’s good to test out some of your other items on enemies and see which, if any, have an effect. You might be surprised what a well-placed hookshot or boomerang can do, or what you can accomplish with some Deku Nuts. A lot of enemies can be stunned, knocked away, or even killed from a distance, no close combat required.

I mean, really, you should just do this anyway. The writing in Ocarina of Time is often silly and worth reading on its own, but characters you speak to will also frequently give you hints about what to do next, or even about secrets you may not have found otherwise. Characters will also adjust their dialogue over time depending on what is going on in the game, so circle back with folks even if you think you’ve heard all they have to say.

And yes, Navi can help, too. She can be a bit grating, but her tips on fighting monsters are a huge help.

Again, this one seems pretty basic, but sometimes you’ll be stuck in a dungeon and have no idea how to solve a puzzle to proceed. I cannot emphasize enough how often the answer is just to look,Â really look around you. Look for weird symbols on the walls, or bits of floor that look funny. Look for fire, or places to light things on fire. Look for switches, or platforms directly below you that you didn’t see before. Look at how things connect to one another. Look underwater. Most of the time if you’re stuck, it’s because you’re not seeing some key element you need to move forward…and often, those things are hidden for a reason.

New to the remake of Ocarina of Time are fairy symbols, odd glowing runes you’ll find inscribed on walls or pillars or other out-of-the-way surfaces as you explore. You can use Navi to examine them. They’ll often give you hints about secrets, or other little bonuses, for interacting with them properly. Again, looking around pays off!

Finally, gold skulltulas. You won’t necessarily know these are important when you first start the game, but they absolutely are. There are 100 of these in the game total, and you get rewards for reaching certain milestones of collecting them. Many of these rewards are very, very good. You’ll want to listen for a scritchy-scratching noise as you explore, that indicates there’s one nearby. You can also find them by rolling into trees that seem to be shedding their leaves. In towns, you’ll only ever find them at night (in dungeons, they’re around all the time). Grab as many as you can!

The Cuccos are the chickens. Don’t attack them. You can pick them up, carry them around, and throw them. But don’t HIT them.

Men Are Outspending Women on Botox. Here’s What They’re Asking For

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Guys are going to increasingly wild lengths to achieve their version of the idealized man. Looksmaxxing, as we’ve come to know it, has included surgical procedures to reshape their eyes, taking a hammer to their face to create a more chiseled jawline, and breaking their legs to gain a couple inches. But an OG in cosmetic procedures is seeing a resurgence in men, too.

Chances are you’re familiar with Botox: localized injections to smoothen wrinkles in common areas like the forehead, the eyes, or around the mouth. Long viewed as a treatment limited to the ladies, it’s increasingly becoming another form of male upkeep. Now we’re learning that men are outspending women on the popular procedure. According to an October 2026 report by Guidepoint Qsight, a medical aesthetics data and intelligence provider, men spent an average of $529 per neurotoxin visit in Q3 of 2026, compared with $455 for women. (The figures include not just Botox but also competitors like Daxxify and Xeomin.)

The potential explanation is simple: Botox is an easy way to look a little younger, more well-rested, and even less angry or severe. (As a built-in bonus, it’s virtually painless—especially compared to the barbaric looksmaxxing trends circulating as of late.) According to a 2025 survey of 2,900 aesthetic plastic surgeons, men accounted for over 15% of all Botox cases that year. It was also their top non-surgical treatment by a landslide.

The growing cohort of men opting into “Brotox” believe it’s money well spent. Nancy Samolitis, MD, a board-certified dermatologist in Los Angeles and co-founder of facile, has been treating men for years but has recently seen an uptick in male clientele, aged anywhere from 35 to 70. These days, “they’re more interested in skincare and more open to procedures,” she says. Many also book their first treatment nudged by their spouse or significant other who are existing patients. Others come in simply wanting to look better but not knowing what treatment to get. In many cases, Botox fits the bill.

While the uninitiated might think the injections make guys look surprised or less masculine, good work can be undetectable. Still you, just fresher.

Kyle Waldrop, a 37-year-old product designer in Los Angeles, first started getting Botox three years ago. “I’ve always been good about keeping up with a skincare routine and taking care of myself, so Botox felt like a natural extension of what I was already doing,” he says. His goal isn’t to change his face but to slow down wrinkles before they become obvious. He gets Botox for his forehead lines and crow’s feet, and recently started treating his neck lines, too.

But it’s not always about looking younger. Men are also using Botox in less obvious places. David Kim, MD, board-certified dermatologist and the founder of Soho Dermatology in New York City, treats the chin for more projection, as well as the underarms to combat excess sweating. Bell Yoo, NP, a board-certified nurse practitioner and the founder of Mimo Aesthetics in West Hollywood, adds that she injects the masseter (and sometimes temporalis) muscles for guys who struggle with jaw clenching or teeth grinding.

Aesthetically, men usually aren’t looking for the same thing from Botox as women. For starters, many want to soften their lines without erasing them. Preserving some natural movement is key. “My female patients often love that smooth, ‘glass skin’ appearance, whereas some of my male patients think it looks too waxy or oily,” Yoo says. Men also tend to opt out of a Botox brow lift, which many women request. “Men would much rather maintain a slightly heavier, lower-set brow than end up with an overly arched or ‘Spock’ brow,” Yoo says. Per Samolitis, this requires different injection points and doses tailored for guys. Kim, meanwhile, is careful not to overdo the crow’s feet. “It looks more natural when men have lines around the eyes, and it makes their face look better overall,” he says. These techniques quell the fears that guys have about looking less masculine or overdone. For most, the ultimate goal is to look relaxed and rejuvenated—not tight and taut. “When it’s done well and with the right amount, you look like yourself, just a bit more rested,” Waldrop says. “I still have all my natural movement and expressions, which is important to me.”

All that subtlety comes at a price. Men may end up paying more than women to treat the same areas, but the higher price tag is actually expected: Men often have larger, stronger facial muscles, which require more units to achieve their desired results, Yoo says.

While more men are embracing Botox, some remain tight-lipped about it. Yoo says that most of her male Botox patients are pretty discreet. “I think there’s still some stigma around men receiving cosmetic treatments, and many would rather keep it to themselves,” she says. Waldrop, however, is an open book. “I don’t really see it as something worth hiding or feeling that weird about,” he says. “Guys are already spending money on skincare, haircuts, hair transplants, working out, and all sorts of things to look and feel better, so I don’t really see why Botox should be treated any differently.”

For Waldrop, Botox is less a dramatic intervention than another item on his grooming checklist. He doesn’t see it as something that needs to be announced or apologized for. At the end of the day, “it’s really not that deep,” he says.

New Federal Reserve Research Finds Cutting Merchant Swipe Fees Even Hurts Consumers Who Don’t Earn Rewards

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

You can earn no miles, no points and no cash back from your credit card and still lose if the government cuts credit card merchant swipe fees. Research from two Philadelphia Federal Reserve economists explains why: the money banks collect when you spend helps pay for better borrowing terms. That’s a problem for the retailers’ argument that consumers who don’t collect rewards are simply paying for everyone else’s vacations. I wrote this week about why the legislation threatening card rewards isn’t dead, even with Dick Durbin retiring and Roger Marshall losing his Senate seat. The political sales pitch is that reducing merchants’ payment costs saves consumers money. That isn’t true and has never been true anywhere in the world it’s been tried. It’s cover for retailers who want the benefits of accepting credit cards, but think the government should require the service be offered to them for less money. But credit cards aren’t just a convenient way to make purchases. They also provide financing for those purchases, and the merchant fee helps support that. Even A Card With No Rewards Can Give Something Back Satyajit Chatterjee and Burcu Eyigungor’s Credit Effects of Credit Card Interchange Fees uses bank supervisory data to examine an overlooked part of the debate: what interchange does to credit itself. The June 26 paper was highlighted in a new Bank of Japan summary. In the data, rewards cards have higher credit limits. Cards without rewards have lower interest rates despite higher default rates. They argue that rewards make repayment more attractive because default means losing access to future rewards. And when a bank keeps interchange instead of paying rewards, competition encourages it to offer better rates and limits to attract the customer’s business. They find that both rewards and non-rewards cardholders gain, even after accounting for higher retail prices. A bank earning money when that person buys groceries has another reason to want the account. Eliminate that revenue and the account becomes less attractive at the same borrowing terms. The adjustment could show up in the interest rate, credit limit or card approvals. Limiting interchange hurts access to credit and credit costs for the most vulnerable> Most Cards Earn Rewards—And Most Interchange Already Goes Back Out Rewards aren’t confined to a small group of people flying first class. The Consumer Financial Protection Bureau’s latest market report says that in 2024: 80% of general purpose credit card accounts offered rewards. 92% of spending on those cards was on rewards accounts. And a separate New York Fed study finds that banks receive interchange averaging 1.82% of purchase volume and incur rewards costs averaging 1.57%. That’s about 86% of issuer interchange revenue going back out as rewards expense. Limits on credit card swipe fees actually redistribute from consumers to merchants, even as proponents seek to villify the banks. Points Aren’t The Whole Reason To Use Credit Instead Of Debit If an airline takes your money and doesn’t provide the flight, the card you used matters. So does whether a fraudulent charge has already emptied your checking account while everyone sorts out what happened. Credit cards come with federal billing dispute rights, including for purchases that weren’t delivered as agreed. With a billing error dispute, you can withhold the disputed amount during the investigation. Debit cards have fraud protections, too. But the money can already be gone from your account. Having a claim for reimbursement isn’t the same as keeping your cash available for rent while the claim gets resolved. Then there are the benefits particular cards add: purchase protection against theft or damage, extended warranties, return protection, rental-car coverage and insurance for covered travel disruptions. Interchange helps fund the card’s economics: rewards, fraud losses, dispute handling and benefits. Cutting interchange doesn’t repeal federal consumer protections athough those were imposed on an economic model that included them. And it an change what banks charge for the product, which optional benefits they offer and which customers they want. The ELT Can Probe Ultra-Diffuse Galaxies and Their Dark Matter Mysteries

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

We've been waiting a long time for the Extremely Large Telescope's first light, scheduled for March 2029. Telescopes this large, powerful, and costly always have multiple science goals, and that's true for the ELT. One of its science goals is to study fundamental aspects of the Universe, including dark matter.

The current model of cosmology is called Lambda Cold Dark Matter (Lambda-CDM or ÎCDM.) It's basically the Big Bang model of cosmology. There are, as every space-head knows, unanswered questions about Lambda-CDM, including the fact that we don't even know what dark matter is. (Some don't think it even exists.)

This is where the ELT comes in. One of its proposed instruments, and telescopes like this always have multiple instruments attached, is called NEXUS@SHARP. SHARP stands for Spectrograph for High Angular Resolution Physics, and NEXUS is a Multi-Object Spectrograph designed to work with SHARP.

New research in the journal New Astronomy outlines how NEXUS@SHARP can probe questions about Lambda-CDM. It's titled "SHARP(ening the) view of unresolved globular clusters in ultra diffuse galaxies," and the lead author is Marco Mirabile. Mirabile is a PhD student at INAF-Astronomical Observatory of Abruzzo (INAF-OAAb) and Gran Sasso Science institute (GSSI) in Italy.

"Ultra-Diffuse Galaxies (UDGs) challenge current galaxy formation models within the ÎCDM framework, particularly regarding their dark matter content and formation histories," the authors write. "Globular Clusters (GCs) serve as excellent tracers for probing the halos of these faint systems; however, current spectroscopic facilities struggle to reach the depth required to fully characterize GC populations beyond the local neighborhood."

UDGs are unusual because they're about as large as the Milky Way, but can have thousands of times fewer stars. The stars are spread out and the galaxies are dim. Basically they're too puffy to agree with Lambda-CDM.

This visualization shows how stars are distributed in a simulated UDG. UDGs are as faint as dwarf galaxies, but are spread out like large galaxies like the Milky Way. Astronomers thought that supernovae explosions could've been responsible for spreading out the stars, but that interpretation hasn't endured. Image Credit: Arianna Di Cintio, Chris Brook, NIHAO simulations and HST

A galaxy's size is shaped by its dark matter halo, one of the cosmos' basic structural units. There's an apparent mismatch between the sizes of UDG halos and the sizes of the UDGs themselves. The expected size of the halos shouldn't produce such diffuse galaxies.

A way to understand this is to examine the globular clusters around UDGs. But the problem is, the spectroscopes astronomers have at their disposal now aren't powerful enough to fully characterize the globular clusters around UDGs. NEXUS@SHARP can extend their reach.

"In this work, we present the scientific potential of NEXUS@SHARP, a near-infrared multi-slit spectrograph proposed on the upcoming Extremely Large Telescope (ELT). We demonstrate that NEXUS is uniquely positioned to overcome current observational limitations by combining high spatial resolution with efficient multiplexing," the authors write. Multiplexing means it's a Multi-Object Spectrograph (MOS) that can observe and capture data from many GCs at once. MOSs are common; Keck, the VLT, Gemini, and the JWST all have them, so do many others. But NEXUS@SHARP would be attached to the extraordinarly powerful ELT and its 39.3-metre-diameter segmented mirror.

All galaxies have globular clusters, including our own. Globular clusters are important when studying UDGs because they're brighter than the UDGs that hosts them, and are easier to observe. They also extend outward further than the UDGs, so they can help measure the dark matter halo better. That should let astronomers measure a more accurate mass constraint. Not only that, but the number of GCs can help determine the mass of the halo because their number scales with mass.

Omega Centauri, seen in this image captured with the WFI camera from ESO's La Silla Observatory, is probably the most well-known of the Milky Way's globular clusters. It has as many as 10 million stars. GCs are bright, so when they're satellites of ultra-diffuse galaxies, they serve as good probes of the galaxies. Image Credit: ESO

Another important thing GC spectra tells scientists is their formation history. The spectra show the ages and the metallicities of GCs, which reveals how quickly the stars in the GC formed. That can show astronomers what kind of UDG they're looking at.

Measuring a handful of GCs around a small number of UDGs won't sufficiently characterize the population of either. NEXUS@SHARP will bring more of them within observational rearch, and it won't require much telescope time because NEXUS examines many GCs at once. "We show that NEXUS can efficiently help to characterize the GC population in UDGs across diverse environments â out to 60 to 70 Mpc â with a few hours of ELT time," the authors write. 70 megaparsecs is about 230 light years.

The ELT is a beast of a telescope, and its heart is the 39.3 meter segmented primary mirror. Its instruments, including NEXUS@SHARP if its built, sit on a separate stationary platform. Image Credit: ESO

What this boils down to is two different scenarios for how UDGs form, and what it tells astronomers about dark matter and Lambda-CDM.

"These capabilities will allow for precise measurements of radial velocities and stellar populations, enabling robust dynamical mass estimates and distinguishing between competing UDG formation scenarios," the authors write.

One scenario says that UDGs are failed massive galaxies that were quenched early on. These ones preserved their dwarf-like stellar masses, but lie within more massive halos typical of a regular galaxy. "These galaxies would thus appear red, old, metal-poor, DM dominated, and host unusually rich globular cluster (GC) populations," the authors write.

The second says that UDGs are puffed-up dwarfs. They are genuinely low-mass galaxies that have expanded in space for some reason. "Relative to the previous scenario, such objects are expected to be bluer, exhibit extended star formation histories, and have DM content and GC populations resembling those of typical dwarfs," the authors explain.

NEXUS@SHARP can shed light on these scenarios and help explain how UDGs formed by examining globular clusters around Ultra-Diffuse Galaxies. That's why the authors wrote that ""Globular Clusters (GCs) serve as excellent tracers for probing the halos of these faint systems."

"To shed light on the nature of UDGs, deep spectroscopic observations of a large sample of UDGs are needed, as combining the analysis of GC richness with the kinematic and evolutionary properties of field stars provides a powerful diagnostic for distinguishing between different formation channels," the authors write.

Basically, the dataset gathered by NEXUS@SHARP will have two independent mass measurements for each UDG, both arising from measuring many GCs at once. One mass measurement comes from the velocity dispersion of the GCs, which shows how much mass is required to keep them in orbit. That's dynamical mass.

The second is also from GCs, but measures their total number. That relies on the knowledge that the number of GCs roughly rises linearly with the mass of a UDG's dark matter halo. That's GC-count mass.

This figure from previous research shows how the number of globular clusters (y-axis) correlates with galaxy mass (x-axis). Each orange or blue circle represents the number of GCs in an individual galaxy. Image Credit: Minh Ngoc Le and Andrew P. Cooper 2025 ApJ 978 33

If both of these ways of measuring mass agree, and both are high, that means that the UDG has an overmassive halo compared to its number of stars. That supports the first UDG formation scenario: it's a failed regular galaxy that quenched early.

If the different measurements both agree, but they're low, that supports the second UDG formation scenario: it's a puffed up dwarf inside a normal sized dwarf dark matter halo.

There's another wrinkle. If the dynamical mass is low, but the GC-count mass is high, then the formation of GC was unusually inefficient for some other, environmental reason.

NEXUS@SHARP is just a concept at this point, and the paper just makes the case for building it. If it's built, the results could go a long way to a better understanding of dark matter and Lambda-CDM. Understanding Lambda-CDM is an ongoing effort, and it's being tackled from different directions with different facilities. While Lambda-CDM is still the best match for lots of data about dark matter and dark energy, it doesn't fit everything.

If built, NEXUS@SHARP can chip away at some of the uncertainty around Ultra-Diffuse Galaxies and their dark matter halos, and can help either confirm or contradict Lambda-CDM.

"By enabling the systematic spectroscopic survey of GCs in low-surface-brightness systems, NEXUS@SHARP will act as a definitive tool for constraining the formation channels of the most elusive galaxy populations in the local universe," the authors conclude.

Evan Gough is a science-loving guy with no formal education who loves Earth, forests, hiking, and heavy music. He's guided by Carl Sagan's quote: "Understanding is a kind of ecstasy."

BYD is launching this 5-seat luxury electric SUV next week with over 1,000 km of range

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

BYD’s luxury brand, Denza, calls it “the king of large 5-seater pure electric vehicles.” The new Denza N8 electric SUV will launch next week, offering an impressive CLTC driving range of over 1,000 km (621 miles), massive digital screens, and plenty of premium extras.

After opening pre-orders for the new 5-seat luxury electric SUV last week, Li Hui, Denza’s general manager, said the results so far “have been excellent.”

According to Li, the Denza N8 is “definitely the most anticipated luxury SUV of the year.” Using BYD’s second-generation Blade Batteries, the Denza N8 EV has a pure electric CLTC range of up to 1,003 km (623 miles) on a single charge.

That’s just as much as the N8 plug-in hybrid (PHEV) it’s replacing, which offered a combined CLTC range of 1,003 km (623 miles) on a full tank of gas and a full charge.

While Li confirmed last week that the new EV variant would go on sale in November, he didn’t give a specific date.

Now, it’s official. Denza announced on its Weibo page on Friday that the launch event for the Denza N8 EV will be held on November 12, 2026.

BYD is still offering a limited-time promo that includes a 5,000 RMB credit for those who place a reservation with a 2,000 RMB ($200) deposit.

The Denza N8 measures 5,150 mm long, 1,999 mm wide, and 1,820 mm high, with a 3,075 mm wheelbase. That’s slightly longer than the Kia EV9 and Hyundai IONIQ 9.

It’s available with a single rear-wheel-drive (RWD) or a tri-motor all-wheel-drive (AWD) powertrain. The RWD version uses a single motor producing either 320 kW (429 hp) or 370 kW (496 hp).

The tri-motor Denza N8 uses BYD’s e3 platform. The setup includes three electric motors: a 270 kW (362 hp) front motor and two 310 kW (416 hp) rear motors, for a combined output of 890 kW (1,193 hp).

Regulatory filings earlier this year revealed it will use either a 130.15 kWh or 105.79 kWh BYD Blade 2.0 battery, offering a CLTC range of up to 1,003 km (623 miles) and 800 km (497 miles), respectively.

The Denza N8 also features BYD’s latest Flash Charging technology, letting it recharge from 10% to 80% in just five minutes. It takes just nine minutes to go from 10% to 97%.

Inside, the five-seat electric SUV features a massive 30″ infotainment screen that spans nearly the entire passenger-side dashboard, plus a new 43″ (1.1 meter) panoramic head-up display (PHUD) at the bottom of the windshield.

Like most of its new luxury vehicles, the Denza N8 is loaded with BYD’s latest software and technology.

Added premium features include zero-gravity seating in all rows, a three-spoke steering wheel, wireless phone chargers, and ambient lighting.

The electric SUV is equipped with a roof-mounted LiDAR to support BYD’s God’s Eye 5.0 intelligent driving assistance system, plus its DiSus-A intelligent air suspension system.

BYD has yet to announce prices for the Denza N8 EV, but it will likely be slightly less than the N8L, an extended version that starts at 299,800 RMB ($45,000).

Real Madrid have a knack for ruining Liverpool transfer plans. Sometimes that's swooping in to sign a player from Liverpool, like Trent Alexander-Arnold or Ibrahima Konate.

Or, more usually, it's them signing someone that the Reds have spent quite some time working on a deal for. You can actually look at half their first-team to find such players, from Aurelien Tchouameni to Kylian Mbappe to Jude Bellingham to Eduardo Camavinga to...

But now there's a new and fascinating way that they could ruin a transfer plan. Robbing Liverpool of their big Virgil van Dijk replacement.

There are few players we're higher on than Jacobo Ramon. Our Value Adder data model rates him as one of the most outstanding defenders in Europe last season at just 21 - and he looks even better this term.

Ramon was fantastic again this week as Como thrashed RC Lens 9-1 in the UEFA Champions League. He led the match for defensive actions, won all of his duels, picked up an assist, and played a truly ridiculous 18 line-breaking passes from centre-back.

It's enough that we've got him down as the ideal replacement for Virgil van Dijk - a player who needs signing very soon. Whether Van Dijk renews his contract or not.

But there's a problem. The more impressive Ramon looks, the more likely it is that Real Madrid simply trigger their buyback clause and use him for themselves.

We're still crossing our fingers that they'll sell once they pay that €9m clause next summer. They've invested in Konate and Dean Huijsen, after all, which opens the door to a sale for big money.

And given this is a player Liverpool were reportedly 'blown away' by earlier this year, we think the Reds would be happy to pay big money for him. We're begging them to.

Ramon feels like a generational centre-back, much as Van Dijk did back in 2017. Signing him with the promise of replacing an all-time great surely seduces him, too, let alone the promise of playing alongside Jeremy Jacquet long-term.

But as we say, Real Madrid hold the cards here and they're on course to rob Liverpool of this signing before it's even off the ground.

He Was Spurs’ Record Transfer - Now He’s 29, Without A Club, and Worth Just £1.6m

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Just seven years after becoming Tottenham Hotspur's record signing, Tanguy Ndombele finds himself without a club.

Born in Longjumeau near Paris, Ndombele rose through the ranks at Guingamp and Amiens, before signing for Lyon in 2017. After enjoying two exceptional years with Les Gones, Tottenham forked out a fee worth upwards of £64 million to bring the midfielder to North London.

He went on to make just 91 appearances for the Lilywhites, before a series of uninspiring loan spells resulted in his contract with Spurs being mutually terminated.

Signing a two-year contract at Nice following his departure from the Premier League in 2024, he failed to impress in the South of France and has subsequently been unemployed since being released in the summer.

Having made his debut in Ligue 2 for Amiens at the age of 18, Ndombele was revered as one of the up-and-coming French talents to watch out for. It wasn't long before Lyon came calling, and he was shining on the biggest stage in his home country.

After making 96 appearances for the Ligue 1 giants and helping them reach the Champions League knockout stages in 2018/19, Ndombele attracted interest from several of Europe's biggest clubs in the 2019 summer transfer window. Tottenham reportedly fended off competition from the likes of Real Madrid and Juventus to secure the signature of the then 22-year-old, who was also beginning to establish himself in the French national team under Didier Deschamps.

The North Londoners, who had just reached a Champions League final, paid a premium for the number eight. The £64 million fee surpassed their previous club record signing, Davinson Sanchez, who had joined from Ajax two years earlier for around £38 million.

This substantial outlay came with hefty expectations, and the pressure this created clearly got to the player. Towards the end of his debut campaign in English football, Spurs boss Jose Mourinho publicly criticised Ndombele for what the manager believed to be a lack of professionalism.

"He's a player with great talent," Mourinho told BBC Sport in 2020.

"He has to know he has to do much better and know I cannot keep giving him opportunities to play because the team is much more important."

This unprofessional tag stuck to Ndombele, with the next few Spurs managers not taking a liking to him either. Ryan Mason, Nuno Espirito Santo and Antonio Conte all granted the player few opportunities to impress (barring a relatively promising 2020/21 season), while persistent fitness issues derailed his time in N17.

He was eventually loaned back to Lyon in an attempt to revitalise his career. After this stint failed, he spent a season each at Napoli and Galatasaray before Tottenham and his representatives agreed to terminate his agreement with the North London club in 2024.

Walking into the job market as a damaged asset, desperately in need of a permanent home, Nice offered him a lifeline back in France in the summer of 2024. Ndombele took this opportunity, signing a two-year deal with the Ligue 1 club.

Despite impressing in his first season under Franck Haise at the Allianz Riviera, the box-to-box midfielder struggled in his second campaign, making just 13 appearances across all competitions. This disappointing second year cost him a new contract, meaning he once again entered the market as a free agent.

He's yet to be offered a lifeline this time around, and TransferMarkt subsequently values the ex-Spurs man at just €2 million (£1.67 million).

The wages of every player at Tottenham for the 2026/27 season, including that of James Maddison and Dominic Solanke.

According to the Sun, Ndombele went on trial at Russian club Rubin Kazan back in October. The Russian Premier League outfit seemingly gave the Frenchman an opportunity to prove that he's still at the level, but there have been no reports that he's been offered a contract since.

The 29-year-old remains available, but the longer he goes without finding a new club, the more difficult it becomes to envisage him returning to the pitch professionally.

Google takes over the Wyoming data centre site Crusoe had planned

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The AI data centre developer is handing over the site a day after Google pledged $17B there.

Google has bought Crusoe’s data centre site in southeast Wyoming and will build the project itself, after pledging $17B there. Bloomberg had reported Crusoe was pushed aside after failing to sign customers. Terms were not disclosed.

Google has bought Crusoe’s data centre site in southeast Wyoming and will build the project itself, Crusoe said on Friday. Neither company disclosed the terms.

The deal comes a day after Google said it would spend $17B on its first Wyoming data centre, in Laramie County. It ends Crusoe’s early work on the site, which included choosing the location, winning government approvals and planning its power supply.

Crusoe announced the project with Blackstone-backed Tallgrass Energy in July 2025, saying it could eventually grow to 10 GW. In June, Crusoe was being pushed aside after failing to sign customers, with Google raising concerns about costs and timing, Bloomberg reported.

Crusoe said it still has more than 6 GW of capacity under contract and over 40 GW in its pipeline. It is also building a data centre campus for Google near Amarillo, Texas, and raised $3.9B at a $30.9B valuation in September. Elsewhere it has landed customers, including deals with Meta in June and a reported $13B contract with Jane Street.

Developers face a tougher climate. Several new governors elected this week have promised to rein in data centres, Bloomberg said, and voters rejected data centres in 40 of 41 local ballot measures.

Gone are the days when holiday sales had a firm beginning and end. These days, if you're not scouring the internet weeks before the actual date of the event, you will miss out on deals. Amazon released some vague official plans for their sale, but there's plenty that's discounted already, many reaching or matching their lowest price ever—we know, because we use tools to verify and track their deals.

While Amazon has released some press releases announcing they will be partaking in the sale (as they do every year), they haven't officially said what dates the sale will take place. Once they do, we'll update you here.

Yes, you will need to be a Prime Member to shop the exclusive sales during Black Friday and Cyber Monday. Prime membership starts at $14.99 per month. While only you can decide if buying a yearly Prime membership is worth it for you, remember you can always sign up and then cancel your Prime membership once the sale is over: Amazon offers a free 30-day trial, so you can shop during both Black Friday and Cyber Monday before canceling without being charged. Here's how to sign up for a Prime account and all of the benefits that come with it.

Rose Lavelle Suits Up to Build Gotham FC’s Future

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Committed to the reigning NWSL champions for three more seasons, the soccer star reflects on leadership, life lessons, and a monumental move to a shiny new stadium in New York City.

Rose Lavelle is breaking down her outfit of the day, though it’s not the ensemble you’d expect from a professional athlete’s social media feed.

“My hard hat, which is… something,” the Gotham FC midfielder says with a chuckle. She looks down to survey the rest of her mandatory construction-site-chic attire. “My gloves—gardener’s. My boots—too big. My vest—cool.”

It’s a Tuesday afternoon in September, and Rose is the first of her teammates to tour the unfinished Etihad Park in Queens, New York, the stadium that Gotham will call home in 2028. Earlier that day, Gotham announced that Rose had signed a contract extension through 2029. In other words, she'll be back when this concrete floor is an actual soccer pitch.

We weave past steel beams and through what will eventually become Gotham's tunnel to the pitch. From there, the grandstands rise around us, a preview of the 25,000-seat venue that will soon welcome Gotham's fans. Rose poses for photos, holding a jersey that says “2029.”

A few days later, Gotham’s president of soccer operations, Yael Averbuch West, tells me she smiled when she saw the images of Rose in her hard hat. The look may be a departure from her usual uniform, but Averbuch West sees something fitting about it. As Gotham prepares to make another postseason run in pursuit of defending their 2025 National Women’s Soccer League title, Rose has become central to the club's plans.

“She has been part of literally constructing this vision with us,” Averbuch West says. “She has been an instrumental part of the vision of the club and our aspirations and our ambition.”

The roughly $780 million Etihad Park is being developed by Major League Soccer's New York City FC, which will share the stadium with Gotham beginning in 2028. It's a significant upgrade from Gotham's current arrangement at Sports Illustrated Stadium in Harrison, New Jersey, where the team uses the visitors' locker room and covers up the New York Red Bulls’ logos with its own banners. At Etihad Park, Gotham will have a locker room of its own, merchandise space in the team store, and digital signage and video boards that can be fully branded for Gotham on match days—no covering up another team's logos required.

The move is another sign of just how much women's professional soccer has changed since Rose entered the NWSL nearly a decade ago. And in that time, Rose has changed, too. At 31, the once up-and-coming star has become a veteran helping set the standard for a club that has no plans to slow down.

The NWSL is not the same league it was when Rose was drafted first overall by the now-defunct Boston Breakers in 2017, playing home games at a 2,500-seat facility at Harvard University. When I sit down with Rose after our stadium tour, she tells me it’s difficult to compare the things that mattered to her as a rookie to signing a contract extension now. “I think of the amount of support staff we had, it was so few—how many people were dedicated towards ticketing and marketing and all of that,” she says. “I notice that stuff a lot more now than I probably did at the time, because you don't know what you don't know.”

Rose has come a long way since then. She won the 2019 World Cup with the U.S. Women’s National Team, earned Olympic gold in Paris in 2024, and navigated injuries and moves between NWSL clubs. She joined Gotham in 2024 and, after ankle surgery sidelined her for the start of her second season, returned to help the club win the 2025 NWSL championship, scoring the winning goal. In January, she was named U.S. Soccer's 2025 Female Player of the Year.

On the field, Rose’s influence isn't always captured in goals or assists. Gotham rookie Andrea Kitahata calls her the club’s “quiet momentum-builder and tone-setter” for how she initiates the team's defensive press and creates attacking opportunities that may not show up on a stat sheet.

Rose has learned plenty from USWNT veterans like Megan Rapinoe, Becky Sauerbrunn, Alex Morgan, Christen Press, and Tobin Heath, who showed her that there was no single way to command respect in a locker room. “Everybody led in their own way,” Rose says. “Everybody's leadership style might have looked different, but it wasn't any more or less impactful than the other.”

Now, the younger players in Gotham’s locker room are learning from Rose—especially since her 2025 ankle surgery, the first of her career, which forced her to find new ways to contribute off the field.

She initially thought surgery would be a straightforward fix. “I think I thought you get surgery to fix it, and when you're off the table, you’re fixed, and it's smooth sailing. And it was not smooth sailing at all,” she says. She pauses. “Yeah, it was really painful! It was really painful.”

The physical recovery was only part of the challenge. Rose says the mental toll of rehabbing—and the uncertainty that came with it—tested her in ways she hadn't anticipated. “It taught me I had a whole ’nother level of mental grind and fortitude that I could battle through,” she says. “I think you learn the most about yourself in the tough moments. I mean, it was hard—but I made it through, and I feel like I gained so much.”

Rose has never been the loudest voice in the locker room, but recent years have taught her that she doesn’t need to be.

Ahead of the 2025 season, Rose told Averbuch West that she would be more intentional about creating the change she wanted to see within the club. “When you're Rose Lavelle, you are a leader, whether you choose to be or not,” Averbuch West says. “It doesn't have to come in big speeches in front of the group. It’s not going to be that. But it's the little things she does behind the scenes, the little connections with players—pulling someone aside, talking on the field.”

Her teammates take cues from her, Averbuch West says. If Gotham is down a goal at halftime, they look to Rose to gauge how she's responding. If Rose is inspired, they’re inspired. And Rose has become increasingly intentional about the example she sets. “She's been very proactive during her time here to share really constructive feedback with us, and to also be part of creating a locker room culture that I think the players at this point are really enjoying,” Averbuch West says.

“Obviously, we had some cheaters in there,” Kitahata jokes. “Rose made sure that she held everyone accountable, made sure no one's looking it up on their phone.”

And Rose isn’t exactly a quiet participant. “She's in the meal room screaming out,” Kitahata recalls, imitating Rose’s clues: “Blank, blank, blank, blank. It's four words. Figure it out, guys!”

She’s equally serious about her teammates’ recovery. During a July road trip to Portland, Rose took Kitahata, still navigating her first professional season, along for a day of mental and physical recovery. They got coffee, browsed the shelves at Powell's Books, and headed to a sauna and cold plunge. A stickler for her recovery routine, Rose insisted they stay submerged up to their necks in the icy water for four minutes. “I'm like, ‘Rose. Rose, I'm freezing!’” Kitahata recalls. But Rose wouldn't budge. “I love that she always holds me accountable for my recovery.” They finished the day with sushi.

Rose is the teammate you want to run into when you’re having a bad day, because she notices, Kitahata says. “Even with a look, she's like, ‘Oh my god, what's wrong? Do we need to go get coffee?’ … It’s very rare to find someone of her stardom that is just so willing to give to the team.”

Rose has become more conscious of how much she'll miss these moments someday. “I'm such a painfully nostalgic person,” she says. “It's like, debilitating sometimes, honestly.”

It's the everyday moments with her teammates that seem to stick with her most. “I was thinking the other day, having moments in the locker room where we have this shared playlist that we're playing, and we're all dancing to it. And I was like, ‘How many grown adults get to dance with their friends in the locker room?’” she says. “It’s little things like that that it's so easy to take for granted, because we get to do it literally every single day, but something that I think is just so special.”

It's also clear how much she values the life she's built at Gotham, and how much she wants to keep being part of it. “I love my life off-field. I love my life on-field. I think we have such an incredible group of players and staff that we get to work with and learn from each day,” she says. “I just felt so content, but also challenged in a way that I felt like I was going to be happily growing.”

For Gotham, the move to Queens isn't just about having a place to call its own. It's also an opportunity to reach a bigger audience. Despite winning two NWSL championships in three seasons, Gotham has struggled to fill the stands in New Jersey consistently. But when the club hosted the Washington Spirit at Citi Field, home of the New York Mets, for its Queens Classic in July, the sellout crowd of 42,175 offered a glimpse of what might be possible across the street at Etihad Park.

The move isn't without its complications. Gotham has deep roots in New Jersey, and some longtime supporters are disappointed to see the club leave. “I was a little sad at first that the team was moving,” says Lindsey Smith, 37, a New Jersey resident and five-year season ticket holder. “But this is so much bigger than a location to me. As someone who wants to make women's sports accessible, I'm very excited for Gotham to move to Queens.”

Smith’s friend Krista Tonnesen, 37, has been attending Gotham matches regularly since 2021. An Astoria resident, that has meant long trips home after night games, sometimes with 40-minute waits between PATH trains. She's thrilled to have Gotham in her borough, and hopeful that the shorter trip will bring more fans into the stands. “The players and the team deserve better,” she says. “I really think moving to New York will turn that around.”

Rose says the atmosphere at the Queens Classic, where she scored the game's only goal, left an impression on her. “To think that we could be playing in front of a crowd and atmosphere like that, every single home game, I think it'll just add that much more to what we're building,” she says.

Back at Etihad Park, as we make our way through the unfinished concrete hallways, I ask Rose if she can picture what it'll be like when Gotham finally takes the field here. “I see the vision,” she says.

She also leads the sports and athletes vertical, traveling to cover the Paris Olympics, Women’s World Cup, WNBA Finals, and NCAA Final Four for WH. She has nearly 15 years of experience writing, editing, and managing social media for national and international publications and is also a NASM-certified personal trainer.
